And I missed my book club last week. It's just... I'll start checking the boxes after lunch, and before I know it, the whole afternoon has gone by. 

And then, well... I get so flustered, and sometimes I've pulled so many papers out to check them that the living room looks a bit untidy. I just couldn't bear to have anyone come over and see the apartment in such a state. It's terribly embarrassing, really. A woman my age ought to have her home in perfect order, shouldn't she? 

I do hope I'm not sounding like a complete recluse. I still keep busy, mostly. Is... is it normal to lose track of time like that when you're just trying to be organized?

I'll start worrying that I might have accidentally thrown away my husband's old tax papers, or... or maybe I slipped a special family recipe into the wrong box. And I just can't seem to let the thought pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. I just have to go make sure I haven't made a mess of things. \n\nDoes that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it properly for you. I know it sounds like such a fuss over nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts cause you a lot of anxiety or distress, and does checking the items actually make you feel better, even if just for a short time?\n\nPatient: Oh my, distress is a rather strong word, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting at home in a panic or anything like that. I really try not to be a bother to anyone with it. It's... it's more of an uncomfortable little itch in my mind. Just a mild sort of worry, really. \n\nBut yes, when I do go and check the folders, it certainly does make me feel better. It puts my mind right at ease just seeing everything exactly where it belongs. It's just... well, sometimes that settled feeling doesn't last quite as long as I'd like, and I feel the need to just peek one more time. \n\nI hope I'm answering your questions alright? I know you must deal with people who have real, serious problems, and here I am fussing over old receipts... I do hope I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend each day checking these things, and does it ever get in the way of doing other things you need or want to do?\n\nPatient: Well... time... it's hard to say exactly, isn't it? Time just sort of slips away when you're busy tidying up. I suppose... well, it might be a bit more than just a few minutes here and there. \n\nI really try not to let it interfere with anything important, I promise you that. But, um... well, I have had to call off a few of my mentoring sessions with the young ladies at the church lately. And I missed my book club last week. It's just... I'll start checking the boxes after lunch, and before I know it, the whole afternoon has gone by. \n\nAnd then, well... I get so flustered, and sometimes I've pulled so many papers out to check them that the living room looks a bit untidy. I just couldn't bear to have anyone come over and see the apartment in such a state. It's terribly embarrassing, really. A woman my age ought to have her home in perfect order, shouldn't she? \n\nI do hope I'm not sounding like a complete recluse. I still keep busy, mostly. Is... is it normal to lose track of time like that when you're just trying to be organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to resist or stop the thoughts about needing to check your papers and boxes, or do you feel like the checking is something you have to do to feel right?\n\nPatient: Oh, I certainly try to just brush them aside. I tell myself, 'Now, you know perfectly well everything is fine.' But... well, like I was trying to explain earlier, even when I absolutely know better, that little thought just keeps needling at me. \n\nI'll try to sit and read, but then the worry creeps in again that I might have accidentally tossed out my husband's old tax papers, or... or maybe put a special family recipe in the completely wrong box. I really do try to stop thinking about it, but I just can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So I suppose... yes, I do end up feeling like I just have to go look, just to prove to myself I haven't been irresponsible. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound too foolish? I know it's silly to fret over such small things, and I really do try my best to be sensible about it. I hope I'm answering exactly what you're asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these thoughts ever take the form of vivid images or impulses that pop into your mind, rather than just being worries about things you might have actually done or might really do?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness no, I wouldn't say I have impulses or anything quite so dramatic as that. I don't want you to think I'm having strange visions or anything untoward. \n\nIt's really just... well, just those little nagging worries I mentioned. Just a thought that maybe I've been a bit careless with my organizing. I don't see pictures in my head or feel like I'm going to do something out of the ordinary. It's all very mundane, really. Just an old woman fretting over her paperwork. \n\nI hope that's alright? I know you must see people with much more vivid imaginations than mine. Am I giving you the right sort of answers for your notes?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Well... oh dear. I suppose if you were to add it all up, it... it probably does amount to more than an hour. Maybe quite a bit more, if I'm being perfectly honest. I feel a bit foolish admitting it, but that's really why I finally decided to come see you.

Time just sort of slips away when you're busy tidying up. I suppose... well, it might be a bit more than just a few minutes here and there. \n\nI really try not to let it interfere with anything important, I promise you that. But, um... well, I have had to call off a few of my mentoring sessions with the young ladies at the church lately. And I missed my book club last week. It's just... I'll start checking the boxes after lunch, and before I know it, the whole afternoon has gone by. \n\nAnd then, well... I get so flustered, and sometimes I've pulled so many papers out to check them that the living room looks a bit untidy. I just couldn't bear to have anyone come over and see the apartment in such a state. It's terribly embarrassing, really. A woman my age ought to have her home in perfect order, shouldn't she? \n\nI do hope I'm not sounding like a complete recluse. I still keep busy, mostly. Is... is it normal to lose track of time like that when you're just trying to be organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to resist or stop the thoughts about needing to check your papers and boxes, or do you feel like the checking is something you have to do to feel right?\n\nPatient: Oh, I certainly try to just brush them aside. I tell myself, 'Now, you know perfectly well everything is fine.' But... well, like I was trying to explain earlier, even when I absolutely know better, that little thought just keeps needling at me. \n\nI'll try to sit and read, but then the worry creeps in again that I might have accidentally tossed out my husband's old tax papers, or... or maybe put a special family recipe in the completely wrong box. I really do try to stop thinking about it, but I just can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So I suppose... yes, I do end up feeling like I just have to go look, just to prove to myself I haven't been irresponsible. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound too foolish? I know it's silly to fret over such small things, and I really do try my best to be sensible about it. I hope I'm answering exactly what you're asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these thoughts ever take the form of vivid images or impulses that pop into your mind, rather than just being worries about things you might have actually done or might really do?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness no, I wouldn't say I have impulses or anything quite so dramatic as that. I don't want you to think I'm having strange visions or anything untoward. \n\nIt's really just... well, just those little nagging worries I mentioned. Just a thought that maybe I've been a bit careless with my organizing. I don't see pictures in my head or feel like I'm going to do something out of the ordinary. It's all very mundane, really. Just an old woman fretting over her paperwork. \n\nI hope that's alright? I know you must see people with much more vivid imaginations than mine. Am I giving you the right sort of answers for your notes?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the time you spend checking your papers and boxes adds up to more than an hour each day?\n\nPatient: Well... oh dear. I suppose if you were to add it all up, it... it probably does amount to more than an hour. Maybe quite a bit more, if I'm being perfectly honest. I feel a bit foolish admitting it, but that's really why I finally decided to come see you. \n\nI just... I find myself spending far too much of my day opening the very same folders and drawers. And those labeled boxes I mentioned. I'll open them again and again, just to make absolutely sure the important papers and family keepsakes are exactly where I left them. Even when I know I just checked them a few minutes before! \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud, doesn't it? I just want to be thorough and keep everything nice and orderly, but I know it's gotten a bit out of hand. I hope you don't think I'm completely losing my wits. Is that... is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Oh, heavens no. I don't have any sort of... well, any special rituals or magic numbers or anything like that. I don't want to give you the wrong impression.

I suppose... well, it might be a bit more than just a few minutes here and there. \n\nI really try not to let it interfere with anything important, I promise you that. But, um... well, I have had to call off a few of my mentoring sessions with the young ladies at the church lately. And I missed my book club last week. It's just... I'll start checking the boxes after lunch, and before I know it, the whole afternoon has gone by. \n\nAnd then, well... I get so flustered, and sometimes I've pulled so many papers out to check them that the living room looks a bit untidy. I just couldn't bear to have anyone come over and see the apartment in such a state. It's terribly embarrassing, really. A woman my age ought to have her home in perfect order, shouldn't she? \n\nI do hope I'm not sounding like a complete recluse. I still keep busy, mostly. Is... is it normal to lose track of time like that when you're just trying to be organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to resist or stop the thoughts about needing to check your papers and boxes, or do you feel like the checking is something you have to do to feel right?\n\nPatient: Oh, I certainly try to just brush them aside. I tell myself, 'Now, you know perfectly well everything is fine.' But... well, like I was trying to explain earlier, even when I absolutely know better, that little thought just keeps needling at me. \n\nI'll try to sit and read, but then the worry creeps in again that I might have accidentally tossed out my husband's old tax papers, or... or maybe put a special family recipe in the completely wrong box. I really do try to stop thinking about it, but I just can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So I suppose... yes, I do end up feeling like I just have to go look, just to prove to myself I haven't been irresponsible. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound too foolish? I know it's silly to fret over such small things, and I really do try my best to be sensible about it. I hope I'm answering exactly what you're asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these thoughts ever take the form of vivid images or impulses that pop into your mind, rather than just being worries about things you might have actually done or might really do?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness no, I wouldn't say I have impulses or anything quite so dramatic as that. I don't want you to think I'm having strange visions or anything untoward. \n\nIt's really just... well, just those little nagging worries I mentioned. Just a thought that maybe I've been a bit careless with my organizing. I don't see pictures in my head or feel like I'm going to do something out of the ordinary. It's all very mundane, really. Just an old woman fretting over her paperwork. \n\nI hope that's alright? I know you must see people with much more vivid imaginations than mine. Am I giving you the right sort of answers for your notes?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the time you spend checking your papers and boxes adds up to more than an hour each day?\n\nPatient: Well... oh dear. I suppose if you were to add it all up, it... it probably does amount to more than an hour. Maybe quite a bit more, if I'm being perfectly honest. I feel a bit foolish admitting it, but that's really why I finally decided to come see you. \n\nI just... I find myself spending far too much of my day opening the very same folders and drawers. And those labeled boxes I mentioned. I'll open them again and again, just to make absolutely sure the important papers and family keepsakes are exactly where I left them. Even when I know I just checked them a few minutes before! \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud, doesn't it? I just want to be thorough and keep everything nice and orderly, but I know it's gotten a bit out of hand. I hope you don't think I'm completely losing my wits. Is that... is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have to perform these checking behaviors in a certain way or a specific number of times to feel reassured or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I don't have any sort of... well, any special rituals or magic numbers or anything like that. I don't want to give you the wrong impression. \n\nIt's not as if I have to tap the box three times or... or count to ten before I open a folder. And I certainly don't think anything dreadful is going to happen, like a fire or a terrible accident, just because I didn't check. It's nothing quite so dramatic. \n\nI just... I just look until I feel certain I haven't been careless. Until I can tell myself, 'See, you put it in the right place, everything is fine.' It's just about being thorough, really. \n\nI do hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I was doing anything terribly bizarre. I'm just trying to be a responsible person, even if I go a bit overboard sometimes. Is that... does that answer your question alright?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know it sounds like such a fuss over nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts cause you a lot of anxiety or distress, and does checking the items actually make you feel better, even if just for a short time?\n\nPatient: Oh my, distress is a rather strong word, isn't it? I wouldn't want you to think I'm sitting at home in a panic or anything like that. I really try not to be a bother to anyone with it. It's... it's more of an uncomfortable little itch in my mind. Just a mild sort of worry, really. \n\nBut yes, when I do go and check the folders, it certainly does make me feel better. It puts my mind right at ease just seeing everything exactly where it belongs. It's just... well, sometimes that settled feeling doesn't last quite as long as I'd like, and I feel the need to just peek one more time. \n\nI hope I'm answering your questions alright? I know you must deal with people who have real, serious problems, and here I am fussing over old receipts... I do hope I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend each day checking these things, and does it ever get in the way of doing other things you need or want to do?\n\nPatient: Well... time... it's hard to say exactly, isn't it? Time just sort of slips away when you're busy tidying up. I suppose... well, it might be a bit more than just a few minutes here and there. \n\nI really try not to let it interfere with anything important, I promise you that. But, um... well, I have had to call off a few of my mentoring sessions with the young ladies at the church lately. And I missed my book club last week. It's just... I'll start checking the boxes after lunch, and before I know it, the whole afternoon has gone by. \n\nAnd then, well... I get so flustered, and sometimes I've pulled so many papers out to check them that the living room looks a bit untidy. I just couldn't bear to have anyone come over and see the apartment in such a state. It's terribly embarrassing, really. A woman my age ought to have her home in perfect order, shouldn't she? \n\nI do hope I'm not sounding like a complete recluse. I still keep busy, mostly. Is... is it normal to lose track of time like that when you're just trying to be organized?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to resist or stop the thoughts about needing to check your papers and boxes, or do you feel like the checking is something you have to do to feel right?\n\nPatient: Oh, I certainly try to just brush them aside. I tell myself, 'Now, you know perfectly well everything is fine.' But... well, like I was trying to explain earlier, even when I absolutely know better, that little thought just keeps needling at me. \n\nI'll try to sit and read, but then the worry creeps in again that I might have accidentally tossed out my husband's old tax papers, or... or maybe put a special family recipe in the completely wrong box. I really do try to stop thinking about it, but I just can't seem to let it pass without feeling like I've done something terribly careless. So I suppose... yes, I do end up feeling like I just have to go look, just to prove to myself I haven't been irresponsible. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound too foolish? I know it's silly to fret over such small things, and I really do try my best to be sensible about it. I hope I'm answering exactly what you're asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these thoughts ever take the form of vivid images or impulses that pop into your mind, rather than just being worries about things you might have actually done or might really do?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness no, I wouldn't say I have impulses or anything quite so dramatic as that. I don't want you to think I'm having strange visions or anything untoward. \n\nIt's really just... well, just those little nagging worries I mentioned. Just a thought that maybe I've been a bit careless with my organizing. I don't see pictures in my head or feel like I'm going to do something out of the ordinary. It's all very mundane, really. Just an old woman fretting over her paperwork. \n\nI hope that's alright? I know you must see people with much more vivid imaginations than mine. Am I giving you the right sort of answers for your notes?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the time you spend checking your papers and boxes adds up to more than an hour each day?\n\nPatient: Well... oh dear. I suppose if you were to add it all up, it... it probably does amount to more than an hour. Maybe quite a bit more, if I'm being perfectly honest. I feel a bit foolish admitting it, but that's really why I finally decided to come see you. \n\nI just... I find myself spending far too much of my day opening the very same folders and drawers. And those labeled boxes I mentioned. I'll open them again and again, just to make absolutely sure the important papers and family keepsakes are exactly where I left them. Even when I know I just checked them a few minutes before! \n\nIt sounds so silly when I say it out loud, doesn't it? I just want to be thorough and keep everything nice and orderly, but I know it's gotten a bit out of hand. I hope you don't think I'm completely losing my wits. Is that... is that the sort of thing you were asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have to perform these checking behaviors in a certain way or a specific number of times to feel reassured or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I don't have any sort of... well, any special rituals or magic numbers or anything like that. I don't want to give you the wrong impression. \n\nIt's not as if I have to tap the box three times or... or count to ten before I open a folder. And I certainly don't think anything dreadful is going to happen, like a fire or a terrible accident, just because I didn't check. It's nothing quite so dramatic. \n\nI just... I just look until I feel certain I haven't been careless. Until I can tell myself, 'See, you put it in the right place, everything is fine.' It's just about being thorough, really. \n\nI do hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I was doing anything terribly bizarre. I'm just trying to be a responsible person, even if I go a bit overboard sometimes. Is that... does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange or rearrange your papers or items in a specific order or way, beyond just checking to make sure they’re there?\n\nPatient: Oh, no... no, I don't think I do that. Once the papers are in their proper folders, I don't feel any need to... well, to line them up perfectly or organize them in a special pattern or anything like that. \n\nIt's really just about making sure they haven't gone missing, just like we talked about. As long as they are safely in the box where they belong, I'm quite satisfied. I certainly don't sit there arranging them by size or color or anything fussy like that! \n\nI hope I'm not being difficult by saying no to that? I know you're asking these questions for a very good reason, and I do want to be as helpful as I can. I just wouldn't want to mislead you into thinking I have habits that I don't actually have. Am I still doing alright with my answers?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Just an old woman fretting over her paperwork. \n\nI hope that's alright? I know you must see people with much more vivid imaginations than mine. Well... I suppose it has, a little bit. I... I really don't like to admit this, because it sounds so dreadful, but... there have been times when I've had to cancel my mentoring sessions with the young ladies at the church. And... and my book club, too.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes... I suppose that's exactly it. It's... it's just this silly little nagging feeling. I try so hard to just ignore it, to sit down with a cup of tea and my book, but... I just can't seem to settle until I've looked. 

I know it sounds so foolish. I mean, I know perfectly well I just checked the blue folder not five minutes ago, and the birth certificates were right there. But the worry just... it just sort of builds up inside me, and the only way to make it stop is to go back and open the folder, and the drawer, and the boxes again. Just to be absolutely certain. 

I spend... well, I spend rather a lot of my day doing it, I'm afraid. Far more than I should. That's really why I came to see you. I hope... I hope that makes sense? I know it's not very logical of me. Does that help explain it properly for you?

I do hope I'm giving you the information you need.\n\nDoctor: How much time do you spend each day checking these things, and does it ever get in the way of doing other things you need or want to do?\n\nPatient: Well... time... it's hard to say exactly, isn't it? Just an old woman fretting over her paperwork. \n\nI hope that's alright? I know you must see people with much more vivid imaginations than mine. Well... I wouldn't want to use a strong phrase like "out of control." That makes me sound quite unhinged, and I promise you I'm keeping my wits about me as best I can! I don't want you to think I'm losing my faculties.
